5                                                                                                5
                    This paper develops Neural Bellman Operators for policy evaluation
6                                                                                                6
                 and feasible improvement in controlled economies. Centered continuation
7                                                                                                7
                 errors link economic transport to full-policy loss. A constructive neural
8                backend retains the feasible actions attaining its labels and supports im-      8

9                plementation under acquired states and endogenous capacity. We give an          9

10               exact continuous-state compiler that preserves the original policy, includ-     10
                 ing ties, while removing a redundant state-cover factor from its evaluation
11                                                                                               11
                 work. The construction combines classical distance transforms with witness-
12                                                                                               12
                 preserving off-grid evaluation and numerical certification. A priced obser-
13               vation technology determines precision from a certified economic account.       13

14               Direct constrained-policy comparisons distinguish signed cost differences       14

15               from the ability to recoup a controller replacement fee. Matched services in-   15
                 clude uniform and error-driven conventional methods, failed refinements,
16                                                                                               16
                 and three- and four-dimensional nonlinear stress cases. The results separate
17                                                                                               17
                 representation savings, complete work, and economic decisions without
18               attributing a native-envelope capability exclusively to neural encoding.        18

19               Broader applications retain their own comparison conditions.                    19

20                                                                                               20

21                  K EYWORDS : Dynamic programming, Neural approximation, Policy cer-           21
                 tification, Recursive utility, Computational economics.

                                        1. INTRODUCTION
5                                                                                               5

6        An economic decision uses a continuation value because its consequences extend         6

7    beyond the current period. A learned continuation is useful only to the extent             7

8    that the decisions made with it are accurate. Prediction loss, a Bellman residual          8

9    at sampled states, and the value of the policy actually implemented are different          9

10   objects. Their distinction is particularly important when a counterfactual changes         10

11   future policies or when a recursive preference evaluates the continuation generated        11

12   by those policies rather than the optimum.                                                 12

13       This paper develops Neural Bellman Operators (NBOs) as a policy-evaluation and         13

14   feasible-improvement method. An operator takes economic primitives, a current              14

15   policy, and computational resources as inputs. It returns a continuation, a feasible       15

16   policy, and an economic error account, or it records that the requested account was        16

17   not obtained within its cap. The continuation is evaluated against its own fitted          17

18   future. Current actions, policy-specific future values, numerical execution, and the       18

19   cost of verification enter the same account. The construction is not a proposal to         19

20   rename an arbitrary neural predictor as a Bellman operator.                                20

21       There are four parts to the argument. First, only action-dependent continuation        21

22   errors affect a current decision. Their oscillation, rather than their absolute level,     22

23   controls loss. A transport bound identifies when an anchor continuation remains            23

24   valid after the future economy changes. These results connect reuse and refresh            24

25   decisions to economic tolerances. They also preserve the original formulation in           25

26   controlled diffusions, recursive preferences, endogenous preferences, and games,           26

27   subject to the distinct hypotheses of those applications.                                  27

28       Second, a signed residual certificate controls a complete returned policy. Its upper   28

29   bound contains the widths of the residual enclosures, the suboptimality of the             29

30   selected actions, and the terminal enclosure. It is invariant to additive date-specific    30

31   shifts of the fitted continuation. This leads to a useful separation: a policy can be      31

32   accurate even though a coarse verifier does not establish its accuracy. We prove           32
                                 NEURAL BELLMAN OPERATORS                                 3

1    that residual verification can be refined while retaining the original network, the       1

2    original deployed actor, and its original allowance. The resulting bound measures         2

3    improved knowledge of the same policy, not a policy improvement disguised as              3

4    additional verification.                                                                  4

5      Third, certification of each policy relative to the optimum does not rank two           5

6    policies. We construct their cost difference directly. Under expected-cost preferences,   6

7    an own-continuation Bellman telescoping identity produces an unbiased policy-cost         7

8    score with a support width governed by the signed certificate. Coupling innovations       8

9    across policies and enclosing every simulated path gives simultaneous finite-sample       9

10   intervals for the difference in the original continuous-innovation economy. The           10

11   statistical assertion is conditional on frozen policies and the stated independent        11

12   simulation model; outward arithmetic and unresolved action-cell boundaries have           12

13   their own explicit treatment.                                                             13

14     Fourth, a deterministic neural construction connects primitive moduli to a com- 14
15   plete policy tolerance. A minimum of Lipschitz cones, realized exactly by ReLU            15

16   gates, fits each date’s own-future Bellman labels without inflating its Lipschitz         16

17   bound when those labels are inexact. The state, action, and arithmetic budgets are        17

18   specified before construction. This yields finite termination for a defined neural        18

19   backend rather than a claim about arbitrary nonlinear training. A new full matched        19

20   catalogue executes the same resolution ladder for this backend and a conventional         20

21   spline, including every unsuccessful rung and three isolated timing repetitions.          21

22     The action that attains a Bellman label supplies additional information. We retain      22

23   its index through the ReLU minimum circuit and deploy that action when its                23

24   cone is selected. A one-sided policy sandwich then avoids charging the separate           24

25   actor and critic accounts twice. Both the nearest-node neural ablation and the            25

26   spline receive a strengthened certificate. Their complete matched frontiers isolate       26

27   certificate sharpness from first-target work and actual policy cost.                      27

28     A feasible policy must also survive the information and arithmetic used to imple- 28
29   ment it. We give a uniform acquisition-aware theorem that permits discontinuous           29

30   witness selection, uncertain measured states and capacity-dependent repair. A cou- 30
31   pled two-state investment economy realizes all covering, integration and repair op- 31
32   erations explicitly, with a conventional fitted-value comparator. The original scalar     32
     4

1    witness policies receive direct expected-cost comparisons at every common first          1

2    crossing, under a tolerance and simultaneous error account fixed before execution. 2
3    This establishes proximity within the declared decision band, not a signed neural        3

4    ranking. A catalogue-choice theorem makes the result usable when implementation          4

5    charges differ. The complete resource curves and an identical native-envelope con- 5
6    trol isolate what the construction, the certificate and the neural representation each   6

7    contribute.                                                                              7

8        Three further results connect this construction to computation and economic          8

9    implementation. First, a literal cone network repeats a scan over every state label      9

10   whenever a Bellman query evaluates its future continuation. On a tensor cover, we        10

11   compile this network once and answer an arbitrary continuous-state query from the        11

12   cell’s corner data. The compiler carries the original action witness and the original    12

13   tie rule. Consequently the improvement in evaluation work does not come from             13

14   changing the policy being evaluated. Classical separable distance transforms supply      14

15   the nodal computation; the additional policy argument establishes the continuous         15

16   off-grid identity and feasible witness realization. In the two-state construction this   16

17   removes the redundant factor that makes dense cone evaluation scale as N 6 rather        17

18   than N 4 . It does not remove the state cover or give a capability unavailable to the    18

19   identical native envelope.                                                               19

20       Second, we make observation accuracy a priced economic input. A finite-bit           20

21   sensor reports a state cell. Robust repair selects an action feasible throughout that    21

22   cell. The policy theorem then gives an augmented loss account consisting of the full- 22
23   information construction bound, an acquisition term decreasing geometrically in the      23

24   number of bits, and a linear information charge. Exact neighboring-bit comparisons       24

25   identify the precision minimizing this guarantee. This is an information-design          25

26   result for the stated technology, not an assertion that minimizing a bound also          26

27   minimizes the unknown actual loss.                                                       27

28       Third, we evaluate actual constrained-policy cost differences, independently of      28

29   their all-state regret certificates. The two installed controllers face common continu- 29
30   ous innovations and the same observation technology. A prospective replacement           30

31   charge turns simultaneous expected-cost intervals into a decision about whether          31

32   either replacement recoups its cost. That question differs both from the sign of the     32
                                 NEURAL BELLMAN OPERATORS                                   5

1    policy difference and from exact equality. A complete matched construction study,           1

2    an error-driven conventional comparator, and higher-dimensional nonlinear stress            2

3    executions accompany the decision analysis. We charge compilation, adaptive pilots,         3

4    unsuccessful resolutions, repair and output, and report the joint policy-comparison         4

5    service separately. These distinctions permit a positive computational contribution         5

6    to the original NBO program without confusing a sharper certificate, a cheaper              6

7    representation, and a better economic policy.                                               7

8      The numerical implementation starts from economic primitives. In the scalar               8

9    investment economy, it fits a fresh ReLU continuation backward against targets              9

10   using its own previously fitted future network. In the two-capital economy, it trains       10

11   hidden locations in a convex squared-ReLU continuation, with continuous simplex             11

12   actions and continuous uniform innovations. The second experiment includes                  12

13   a flexible nonnegative ridge dictionary as well as a quadratic projection. Each             13

14   comparator constructs its own future targets and uses the same final economic               14

15   criterion. Neither conventional values nor inherited neural weights are used as             15

16   labels for the neural services.                                                             16

17     We report the complete 150-service catalogue, not only successful candidates. In          17

18   the original capped scalar services the neural procedure reaches the all-state target       18

19   0.06 in all twelve cases and 0.04 in six, whereas the spline reaches both targets in        19

20   every case. In the coupled services the neural procedure reaches 0.25 in six cases          20

21   and 0.10 in five; the flexible ridge reaches both in all six. These statements describe     21

22   the original service, including its original verification cap.                              22

23     A subsequent diagnostic holds both the neural weights and the deployed policies           23

24   fixed. Refining only residual verification certifies all six previously unresolved scalar   24

25   policies at 0.04 and the previously unresolved coupled policy at 0.10. The original         25

26   failures are not relabeled. The diagnostic shows that they do not, by themselves,           26

27   identify a failure to construct an accurate neural policy. It does not identify the         27

28   population success probability of the optimizer, establish a new end-to-end runtime,        28

29   or change any policy’s cost.                                                                29

30     The comparative findings are equally integral to the paper. The original scalar           30

31   signed policy comparisons frequently favor the spline. The new coupled direct               31

32   intervals all contain zero and therefore do not order the policies. The precision           32
     6

1    controller genuinely escalates after rejected low-precision proposals, but its com- 1
2    plete recorded work does not improve on fixed binary64 in this experiment. These          2

3    findings distinguish the certified capability of the construction from a comparative      3

4    advantage that would require additional evidence.                                         4

5        The paper retains its original economic subject and full theory. The exposition now   5

6    follows the economic problem, the operator, the policy theorem, and the evidence. 6
7    The complete previous article, proof supplement, and economic applications are            7

8    materialized unchanged within the current source tree as preserved companions; the        8

9    preservation map records their exact identities. No historical theorem or application     9

10   is deleted to obtain a shorter active exposition. The present article and new technical   10

11   supplement state and prove the additional results used by the new evidence.               11

12                                                                                             12

13                                                                                             13

14                                                                                             14
                                   1.1. Relation to the literature
15                                                                                             15

16       Neural Bellman Operators sit within approximate dynamic programming, fitted- 16
17   value methods, and policy iteration, not outside them. Regression-based contin- 17
18   uation methods and simulation-based dynamic programming supply important                  18

19   conventional benchmarks (Judd, 1998, Powell, 2011, Longstaff and Schwartz, 2001,          19

20   Ernst et al., 2005). Actor–critic and approximate policy-iteration methods separate       20

21   evaluation and improvement (Konda and Tsitsiklis, 1999, Scherrer et al., 2015); safe- 21
22   improvement arguments likewise distinguish a proposed action from a verified              22

23   gain (Kakade and Langford, 2002, Pirotta et al., 2013). We use these distinctions to      23

24   organize the economic object returned by the algorithm.                                   24

25       Neural methods for differential equations provide ways to approximate values          25

26   and their derivatives (Sirignano and Spiliopoulos, 2018, Han et al., 2018, Hornik         26

27   et al., 1990). Such approximation does not on its own verify a returned dynamic           27

28   policy or its numerical execution. Our certificates can be applied to neural or           28

29   conventional continuations. The neural-specific construction lies in fresh own-future     29

30   training and, in the coupled experiment, learned hidden locations. Its comparative        30

31   value must be isolated against flexible conventional methods, not inferred from the       31

32   certificate’s validity.                                                                   32
                                 NEURAL BELLMAN OPERATORS                                      7

1      The general economic formulation admits recursive utility (Epstein and Zin, 1989,            1

2    Duffie and Epstein, 1992). The compact-domain policy theorem uses monotonicity                 2

3    and cash invariance, while the original Gaussian entropic construction has separate            3

4    moment and spectral conditions. These are different bridges, not interchangeable               4

5    assumptions. The direct paired-cost identity below applies to conditional expec- 5
6    tation; it is not asserted for a nonlinear certainty equivalent. The confidence step           6

7    uses the empirical Bernstein inequality of Maurer and Pontil (2009). Our additional            7

8    ingredients are the policy-specific telescoping score, its certificate-derived support,        8

9    and the numerical enclosure of the original continuous-law comparison.                         9

26     The applications companion retains the full boundary specifications, infinite- 26
27   horizon transversality conditions, utility-domain restrictions, temporal-self equa- 27
28   tions, and game conditions. A finite-domain residual calculation does not establish            28

29   any of these by itself. In a game, certifying a specified deviation problem is not the         29

30   same as establishing the equilibrium fixed point. In continuous time, the controlled           30

31   generator contains state derivatives; freezing a critic during an actor update does            31

32   not suppress legitimate derivatives of the represented state-dependent function.               32

2    that has not met its target at its cap records failure, with the complete cost of                  2

3    its attempts. The retained factor-training analysis provides a finite constructive                 3

4    theorem for a separate structured class. The nonlinear schedules below instead have                4

5    an explicit, auditable capped-service estimand.                                                    5

1      The proof compares the true and fitted objective at the two selected actions and                     1

2    couples the future states. The weighted finite-menu bound is Cauchy–Schwarz                            2

3    applied to ei − e j . Details are in the technical supplement. An additive continuation                3

4    error common to all actions is irrelevant to the decision. A finite menu, however,                     4

6    actions.                                                                                               6

7      For recursive maps with sup-norm Lipschitz constants β k on a common invariant                       7

8    utility domain, a one-step perturbation νk propagates as bk ≤ νk + β k bk+1 . This is                  8

9    the recursive counterpart of (6). Its use requires the common domain; it cannot cross                  9

10   a singular utility boundary by extending a local constant. These transport bounds                      10

11   retain the original refresh logic: use an anchor while its economic allowance covers                   11

12   approximation and future change, and recertify or recompute when that condition                        12

13   is exhausted.                                                                                          13

7    analogous identity for the fixed-policy map. Starting from g ≥ f T − u T , backward               7

16
     cal experiment is not needed for its validity. Its numerical usefulness depends on                16

17
     producing small, valid enclosures.                                                                17

27     The original actor allowance is valid on the entire state domain, so a finer critic             27

28   verification mesh does not invalidate it. Conversely, replacing it by an allowance for            28

29   a newly optimized fine-grid actor would certify a different policy. The implemen- 29
30   tation records the original actor hash and explicitly discards any fine-grid action               30

31   proposals generated internally by the verifier. Those proposals’ work and transient               31

32   storage are still charged.                                                                        32
     12

1      The corollary does not promise that arbitrary precision is attainable by mesh              1

2    refinement. Nodal residual variation, the retained actor allowance, and the terminal         2

3    enclosure can leave a positive floor. Nor is the corollary a claim that additional           3

4    verification improves welfare. It distinguishes the accuracy of a fixed economic             4

5    decision rule from the sharpness of our knowledge of that accuracy.                          5

7                        3.3. Construction beyond post-training verification                      7

8      The original trainable-factor construction remains a constructive part of NBO. 8
9    For a quadratic own-policy continuation x ′ Pt x + ct , a square-activation network          9

10   ∥Wt x ∥2 /d + cbt trains every entry of Wt . Its population derivative loss and gradient     10

11   are                                                                                          11

12                                                                                                12
                    Lt (W; P) = 14 ∥W ′ W − dP∥2F ,     ∇W Lt = W (W ′ W − dP).
13                                                                                                13

14   If σmin   (W ) ≥ s > 0, then ∥W ′ W/d − P∥   F ≤ ∥∇W Lt ∥ F / ( ds ). Together with the full- 14

15   vector actor defect, this gives a policy-error account for a jointly trained hidden          15

16   factor. The own-policy coefficient is re-evaluated for the returned policy, not silently     16

17   borrowed from an earlier training policy.                                                    17

18     The primitive-budget analysis in the retained article chooses a feasibility witness,       18

19   coefficient envelopes, rank margins, and update budgets before training. In the              19

20   Gaussian entropic extension it also verifies positive exponential-moment margins. 20
21   Under its stated spectral hypotheses, a scaled orthogonal initialization and bounded         21

22   gradient steps give the finite training construction proved there. The finite matrix         22

23   and Gaussian formulas are not a nonlinear dimension-scaling experiment. They                 23

24   also do not imply that Adam or L-BFGS has a deterministic residual cap for a                 24

25   general squared-ReLU network. We preserve both results and keep the two logical              25

26   statements separate.                                                                         26

27                                                                                                27
                    4. FROM ECONOMIC PRIMITIVES TO NONLINEAR CERTIFICATES
28                                                                                                28

29
                              4.1. Backward fitting and selected policies                         29

30     For each method, initialize a fresh terminal fit from g. At date t, construct tar- 30
31   gets from Tt f t+1 using that method’s own already fitted future. Fit the current            31

32   continuation under the frozen schedule. Then propose feasible actions, enclose               32
                                      NEURAL BELLMAN OPERATORS                                   13

1    the Bellman infimum and the selected-action gap, and validate residual coverage. 1
2    Store a checkpoint before reporting its first target crossing. Continue through the               2

3    declared ladder only as specified by the original study. The record includes all                  3

4    attempted resolutions, unsuccessful target queries, target-generation work, and                   4

5    storage operations.                                                                               5

6      The scalar network has 63 ReLU features. Its training uses 513 states, a 257- 6
7    point target action construction, 400 Adam steps at learning rate 5 × 10−5 , and a                7

8    final output fit. The scalar verifier uses its own finer action and state ladders. The            8

9    conventional spline is reconstructed from the same primitives with its own ladder                 9

10   and targets. A conventional target solver is not used to label a neural continuation. 10
11     The coupled neural continuation is                                                              11

12                                                  48                                                 12

13                       f t ( x ) = at + bt′ x +   ∑      ctj (wtj′ x + vtj )2+ ,   ctj ≥ 0.   (11)   13
                                                    j =1
14                                                                                                     14

15   Hidden locations and weights are trained. Nonnegative coefficient initialization and              15

16   a finite L-BFGS schedule of 300 iterations act on fresh own-future targets. The ridge             16

17   comparator instead fixes a 96-feature squared-hinge dictionary and fits nonnegative               17

18   coefficients to its own targets; zero coefficients may be pruned. Its dictionary seed             18

19   is fixed by date, so repeated training-seed labels do not create independent random               19

20   ridge representations. A quadratic projection is retained as a restricted comparator,             20

21   not treated as the strongest conventional alternative.                                            21

22                                                                                                     22

23
                           4.2. All-state and continuous-action verification                           23

24     At each state node, outward arithmetic encloses the actual network value and                    24

25   the action objective. In the coupled economy, convexity in actions and a positive                 25

26   strong-convexity constant yield a continuous-simplex primal–dual gap. A projected                 26

27   optimizer merely proposes the action; its stopping flag is not the certificate. State             27

28   interpolation then extends the signed residual bounds off-grid. The nearest-node                  28

29   deployed actor receives a separate, uniform allowance for using its node action at                29

30   the actual state.                                                                                 30

31     Uniform innovations are integrated through positive-part moments, rather than                   31

32   replaced by a sampled innovation law. The elementary identity for a uniform                       32
     14

1    variable U on [−h, h] is                                                                    1

2                                                                                                2
                                      (y + h)3+ − (y − h)3+
3                       E(y + U )2+ =                       ,         h > 0.                     3
                                                 6h
4                                                                                                4

5
     Successive integration gives the corresponding formula for independent coordi- 5
6
     nates and affine neuron arguments; zero coefficients are handled by the lower- 6
7
     dimensional formula. Interval evaluation encloses cancellation and rounding. The            7

8
     supplement specifies the mesh and action bounds used in the inherited verifier.             8

9
       The residual account distinguishes a nodal enclosure from state coverage, actor           9

10
     error, and terminal error. A nodal enclosure contains the effects of representation,        10

11
     fitting, Bellman optimization, and arithmetic; it is not an identified decomposition        11

12
     into a pure approximation error and a pure optimizer error. In particular, sub- 12
13
     tracting a training loss from the residual does not estimate either component. The          13

14
     diagnostic reports every separately available allowance without counting nested             14

15
     subcomponents twice.                                                                        15

16                                                                                               16

17                              4.3. Work, dimension, and execution                              17

18     For tensor state, action, and innovation covers of sizes Kx , Ka , m, a direct verifier   18

19   pays work of order                                                                          19

20                                                                                               20

21                   ∑ Kx Ka {m(CF + Pt+1 ) + Cρ } + ∑ Kx Pt + Wstorage,                  (12)   21
                     t< T                               t≤ T
22                                                                                               22

23   where Pt counts network evaluation work. Actor storage is of order TKx d a . Analytic       23

24   integration replaces m by the work of the moment formula, not by zero. With                 24

25   tensor covers, Kx scales as h− dx
                                  x ; analogous factors arise for action and innovation          25

26   discretizations. A dimension-explicit certificate is therefore not a dimension-free         26

27   algorithm.                                                                                  27

28     If a Lipschitz innovation approximation contributes at most ζ t to Qt , widening          28

29   residual endpoints by ζ t and the actor allowance by 2ζ t adds 4 ∑t Bt ζ t to (8). This     29

30   is a discretization account, not permission to replace a continuous law without             30

31   charging its error. Independent policy queries may have their own scenario-tree or          31

32   simulation cost and are recorded separately.                                                32
                                       NEURAL BELLMAN OPERATORS                                              15

1      The complete service includes own-future targets, fitting, unsuccessful refine- 1
2    ment, action extraction, verification, applicable policy queries, serialization, and                          2

3    durable checkpoint storage. Initialization and outer-process time are retained sepa- 3
4    rately. New local diagnostic clocks are not added to old GitHub-hosted clocks to                              4

5    manufacture an end-to-end estimate. All reported arithmetic certificates concern                              5

6    stored binary coefficients with outward enclosures. The inherited execution account                           6

7    treats rounded state acquisition and transition/cost evaluation separately from                               7

8    ideal policy loss.                                                                                            8

       To evaluate the original continuous law, simulate uniform innovation bins and

9                                                                                                        9

10                                                                                                       10

11                                                                                                       11

12                                                                                                       12

13                                                                                                       13

28     The coverage statement is conditional on frozen training outputs and an in- 28
29   dependent simulation model. Fixed PCG64 streams make this implementation                            29

30   reproducible; a fixed stream is not itself a proof of probabilistic independence. No                30

1                 6. INVESTMENT ECONOMIES AND THE COMPLETE EVIDENCE                                    1

2                                                                                                      2
                                       6.1. Design and provenance
3                                                                                                      3

4      The original science design has twelve scalar neural services, twelve scalar                    4

5    spline services, six coupled neural services, six coupled ridge services, six coupled             5

6    quadratic services, and 36 services for each of three precision rules. The successful             6

7    source-bound execution contains all 150 records. The original first run, with four                7

8    nonfinite-network exceptions, is retained. Its numerical inverse-softplus initializa- 8
9    tion was subsequently replaced by an algebraically equivalent stable expression;                  9

10   economic primitives, seeds, targets, budgets, and comparators were unchanged. 10
11   We do not treat the failed first run as nonexistent or combine its clocks with the                11

12   successful run.                                                                                   12

13     The R44 protocol was committed before the additional diagnostics were executed. 13
14   It is a post-review protocol, not an original preregistration. It selects the seven               14

15   unresolved tight-target neural objects by their already observed outcomes and                     15

16   freezes their weights and policies. The paired comparison uses all six coupled pairs              16

17   and all four originally declared states. A source manifest binds the original inputs,             17

18   the diagnostic code, outcomes, and publication sources. Every original record and                 18

19   all five original science-source hashes were replayed.                                            19

20                                                                                                     20

21                                                                                                     21
                                   6.2. Scalar and coupled primitives
22                                                                                                     22

23     The scalar experiment retains the original bounded investment state, price and                  23

24   risk cells, three declared training seeds, continuous feasible action interval, and the           24

25   independent own-policy evaluator. Prices are one and four. Both methods construct                 25

26   their own continuations from the same primitive problem. The retained evidence                    26

27   and code specify every scalar primitive; the full economic derivation remains in the              27

28   original investment section and applications.                                                     28

20   inadequate policies. All seven selected policies admit the tighter certificates when       20

                                 6.7. What the evidence identifies
5                                                                                                5
       The evidence identifies a finite from-primitives nonlinear construction, all-state
6                                                                                                6
     certificates for its returned policies, a useful retained-policy verification refinement,
7                                                                                                7
     and direct economic comparisons under the declared state and innovation design.
8                                                                                                8
     It also identifies the continued strength of conventional comparators. It does not
9                                                                                                9
     identify a general neural speed advantage, a nonlinear high-dimensional frontier,
10                                                                                               10
     or a population probability of optimizer success.
11                                                                                               11
       An adaptive sparse-grid or adaptive-partition comparator has not been exe-
12                                                                                               12
     cuted for the original neural optimizers. Section 15 adds a separate error-driven
13                                                                                               13
     coordinate-FVI comparator and three- and four-dimensional stress catalogue for
14                                                                                               14
     the constructive backend; neither retroactively changes the optimizer study. Neither
15                                                                                               15
     has a nonlinear dimension frontier or repeated isolated timing of every original
16                                                                                               16
     scalar and coupled service. Section 7.3 adds a separate predeclared horizon/price
17                                                                                               17
     catalogue with repeated isolated timing for the deterministic constructive backend
18                                                                                               18
     and spline; it does not replace the original experiments. The retained dimension-
19                                                                                               19
     explicit analysis exposes the tensor factors those studies would have to confront,
20                                                                                               20
     rather than treating a two-state experiment as their substitute. The general eco-
21                                                                                               21
     nomic framework and conditional constructive results remain in force; the missing
22                                                                                               22
     experiments concern comparative evidence for broader implementation claims.
23                                                                                               23

24                                                                                               24
             7. A CONSTRUCTIVE NEURAL SERVICE WITH A PRIMITIVE ERROR BUDGET
25                                                                                               25

26     The policy theorem is deliberately independent of the representation of the con- 26
27   tinuation. A construction theorem must do more: it must produce a representation,           27

28   feasible actions, and verified residuals from economic primitives. This section             28

29   supplies such a theorem for a deterministic ReLU backend. It does not assume                29

30   convergence of the nonlinear optimizers used in the earlier experiments. Its distin- 30
31   guishing property is that the continuation has a prescribed Lipschitz constant even         31

32   when its Bellman labels are inexact.                                                        32
                                      NEURAL BELLMAN OPERATORS                                            23

1                       7.1. Primitive conditions and the neural architecture                                   1

2                                                                                                               2
       Consider the compact Bellman economy of Section 2, with a common action set
3
     A ⊂ Ra and state set X ⊂ Rd . All distances in this section use the ℓ1 norm. The                           3

4                                                                                                               4
     innovation law and the monotone, cash-invariant functional ρt do not depend
5                                                                                                               5
     on the state or action. The transition remains in X. Suppose known nonnegative
6                                                                                                               6
     constants satisfy, uniformly in the innovation,
7                                                                                                               7

8                             |ct ( x, a) − ct (y, b)| ≤ Cx,t ∥ x − y∥1 + Ca,t ∥ a − b∥1 ,               (17)   8

9                    ∥ Ft ( x, a, z) − Ft (y, b, z)∥1 ≤ Fx,t ∥ x − y∥1 + Fa,t ∥ a − b∥1 .                (18)   9

10                                                                                                              10

11   The terminal cost is L g -Lipschitz. These assumptions are additional conditions                           11

12   on the constructive backend, not a replacement for the more general feasible cor- 12
13   respondences in the original economic formulation. A state-dependent feasible                              13

14   correspondence requires its own feasible-cover and transport constants.                                    14

15     We require an effective numerical description of the primitives: certified finite                        15

16   state and action covers can be obtained at every positive radius; their nodes are                          16

17   feasible; and a numerical evaluator can enclose each Bellman query to any requested                        17

18   positive tolerance in finite work. The last condition includes evaluation of ρt , not                      18

19   just evaluation of the network. For expectation with a compact innovation domain,                          19

20   known cell probabilities and a certified innovation cover provide one such evaluator. 20
21   If Ft is Fz,t -Lipschitz in the innovation, replacing each cell by a representative at                     21

22   distance at most hz contributes at most β t Lt+1 Fz,t hz . Arithmetic, probability-mass                    22

23   approximation, and any remaining integration error must be added. The experiment                           23

24   below instead integrates a piecewise-linear network analytically under the original                        24

25   continuous uniform law.                                                                                    25

26     Set                                                                                                      26

27                LT = Lg ,       Lt = Cx,t + β t Fx,t Lt+1 ,             Lta = Ca,t + β t Fa,t Lt+1 .   (19)   27

28                                                                                                              28
     Choose state nodes xt,i of covering radius ht and action nodes at,j of covering radius
29                                                                                                              29
     k t . Given the continuation already constructed at date t + 1, compute numbers qbt,ij
30                                                                                                              30
     satisfying
31                                                                                                              31

32                                    |qbt,ij − Qt ( f t+1 ; xt,i , at,j )| ≤ et .                       (20)   32
     24

1    Define yt,i = min j qbt,ij , choose a minimizing action index j(t, i ) with a fixed tie rule,            1

2    and construct                                                                                            2

3                                                                                                             3
                                       f t ( x ) = min{yt,i + Lt ∥ x − xt,i ∥1 }.                      (21)
4                                                 i                                                           4

5    At date T, use labels within eT of g and the same formula with constant L g . At any                     5

6    other date, deploy the action at,j(t,i( x)) attached to a nearest state node, again with                 6

7    a fixed tie rule. The action is feasible by construction. No future optimal value or                     7

8    conventional comparator is used as a label.                                                              8

9      Formula (21) is an exact ReLU construction, not an appeal to a universal approxi- 9
10   mation theorem. Absolute values use |u| = max{u, 0} + max{−u, 0}; minima use                             10

11   min{u, v} = u − max{u − v, 0}. A balanced tree of these gates implements the finite                      11

12   minimum. With Kt state nodes, the resulting sparse affine–ReLU circuit has O(dKt )                       12

13   coefficients and O(1 + log Kt ) depth with unrestricted affine fan-in. Signed interme- 13
14   diate values can be carried by their positive and negative parts. The construction is                    14

15   related to the classical Lipschitz extension formula of McShane (1934); neither that                     15

16   formula nor its elementary ReLU realization is claimed as new. Here its role is to                       16

17   close an own-future dynamic construction, including the deployed actor and every                         17

18   numerical allowance.                                                                                     18

19                                                                                                            19
          T HEOREM 5—Constructive own-future neural policy: Under the preceding condi-
20                                                                                                            20
     tions, the network (21) is Lt -Lipschitz at every date, independently of the numerical label
21                                                                                                            21
     errors. Its residual and its deployed actor satisfy
22                                                                                                            22

23
                                                      −et ≤ f t − Tt f t+1 ≤ Lta k t + et + 2Lt ht ,   (22)   23

24                                                                                                            24
                 Qt ( f t+1 ; x, πt ( x )) − Tt f t+1 ( x ) ≤ Lta k t + 2et + 2Lt ht .                 (23)
25                                                                                                            25

26                                                                                                            26
     At the terminal date, −eT ≤ f T − g ≤ eT + 2L g h T . Consequently the cost of the returned
27                                                                                                            27
     policy, relative to the optimum over all admissible adapted policies, is uniformly bounded by
28                                                                                                            28

29                    Gcon = ∑ Bt {2Lta k t + 4et + 4Lt ht } + BT {2eT + 2L g h T }.                   (24)   29
                                t< T
30                                                                                                            30

31   For fixed nodes and Lt , changing every label by at most δ changes the represented function              31

32   by at most δ in the uniform norm; it does not change the Lipschitz bound.                                32
                                     NEURAL BELLMAN OPERATORS                                        25

1      P ROOF : Monotonicity and cash invariance imply nonexpansiveness in the                             1

2    uniform norm. Thus Qt ( f t+1 ; ·, a) and Tt f t+1 are Lt -Lipschitz, while the action                2

3    modulus is Lta . At a node, minimization of the numerical queries gives −et ≤ 3
4    yt,i − Tt f t+1 ( xt,i ) ≤ Lta k t + et . Each cone in (21) is Lt -Lipschitz, as is their finite min- 4
5    imum. The lower residual bound follows from the triangle inequality for every                         5

6    cone; the upper bound uses a nearest node twice, once for the cone and once                           6

7    for the Bellman value. At that node the selected action is within Lta k t + 2et of the                7

8    continuous-action infimum. Transport to x costs at most 2Lt ht . The terminal argu- 8
9    ment is identical without action minimization. Substitution of these widths and                       9

10   actor allowances into Theorem 2 gives (24). Finally, minima of two arrays whose                       10

11   entries differ by at most δ differ by at most δ. The supplement gives the construction,               11

12   measurability, and work details.                                                           Q.E.D.     12

14
       C OROLLARY 6—Deterministic termination at an economic tolerance: Let ε > 0,                         14

15
     H = ∑tT=0 Bt , and s = ε/H. At nonterminal dates choose                                               15

16                                                                                                         16
                             et ≤ s/8,        Lta k t ≤ s/8,     Lt ht ≤ s/16.                      (25)
17                                                                                                         17

18   At the terminal date choose eT ≤ s/4 and L g h T ≤ s/4. Terms with zero coefficient impose            18

19   no restriction. Then the finite constructive service returns a feasible neural policy with loss       19

20   at most ε. No probability law over initializations and no optimizer stopping criterion is             20

21   needed.                                                                                               21

22                                                                                                         22

23     The result follows because each discounted bracket in (24) is at most s. Effec- 23
24   tive compactness and the numerical evaluator give finite work for these choices. 24
25   Arbitrarily small tolerances may require arbitrary precision; the corollary does                      25

26   not promise termination in fixed binary64. A finite implementation ladder can                         26

27   still return a recorded failure, even though the unbounded constructive algorithm                     27

28   terminates under the stated hypotheses.                                                               28

29     The earlier scalar constructive result in the retained paper implemented piecewise- 29
30   linear interpolation as a ReLU network. Its sufficient allocation controlled a label- 30
31   error-to-mesh ratio in the propagated slope. Formula (21) works in arbitrary finite                   31

32   state dimension and enforces the slope bound directly. In particular, label and                       32
     26

1    action allowances can be allocated at order ε without a separate slope-preservation     1

2    requirement that those allowances be quadratic in the state spacing. This is an         2

3    improvement of that sufficient proof budget, not a lower bound for all spline           3

4    methods or evidence that conventional Lipschitz projection is unavailable.              4

                                 7.2. What the construction costs
7                                                                                            7

8      The construction does not eliminate state coverage. With Kt state nodes, Jt action    8

9    nodes, Mt innovation representatives, and network evaluation cost Pt+1 , direct         9

10   target formation requires                                                               10

11                                                                                           11
                                                                               !
12                                                                      T                    12

13
                     ∑ Kt Jt { Mt (CF,t + Pt+1 ) + Cρ,t + Cc,t } + O   ∑ dKt          (26)
                                                                                             13
                    t< T                                               t =0
14                                                                                           14

15   arithmetic operations under the indicated evaluation model. Cρ,t includes the finite- 15
16   risk functional and probability processing. Certified integration can replace the       16

17   innovation sum but its own cost must be charged. Bit lengths, adaptive arithmetic,      17

18   and nearest-node policy queries are additional explicit costs, not constants sup- 18
19   pressed by a dimension claim. A rectangular state cover with radius ht can require      19

20   order h− d                                                     −a
            t nodes; a rectangular action cover can require order k t actions. The theo- 20
21   rem is therefore a dimension-explicit construction, not an assertion that a nonlinear   21

22   high-dimensional frontier has been executed or that the curse of dimensionality has     22

23   been removed.                                                                           23

24     In one state dimension, the finite envelope can be compiled exactly into a            24

25   piecewise-linear representation. Two passes compute the greatest L-Lipschitz mino- 25
26   rant of the node labels. Between neighboring nodes the envelope is the minimum          26

27   of the two adjacent affine cones. At most one additional intersection is needed per     27

28   interval. Rational knots and values permit exact antiderivatives; an interval evalu- 28
29   ator encloses their numerical evaluation. This compilation evaluates the defining       29

30   network exactly and does not substitute labels from a separately solved Bellman         30

31   problem. The spline comparator receives the same exact-integration acceleration, so     31

32   it is not charged for an artificial dense neural evaluation loop.                       32

10   The invariant domain follows from monotonicity in x, a, z and the two extreme           10

11   transitions 0 and 1. Valid primitive state moduli are Cx = 23/8, Fx = 3/4, and          11

12   L g = 17/4; action moduli are Ca = p/2 + 1/4 and Fa = 1. The downside term              12

13   penalizes a low capital stock, and the quartic investment cost discourages rapid        13

14   adjustment. This is a numerical economic laboratory, not an estimated or calibrated     14

15   quantitative exercise.                                                                  15

16     The complete design was fixed before execution: horizons {2, 4}, prices {1, 4},       16

17   two methods, three isolated repetitions, and the full common state/action ladder        17

18   N = A ∈ {16, 32, 64, 128, 256, 512}. The targets are 0.5, 0.25, and 0.125 in units of   18

19   discounted cost. These are policy-loss tolerances, not equivalence margins for a        19

20   neural-minus-spline contrast. Every rung restarts from terminal primitives and          20

21   constructs its own future backward. The neural method uses (21); the spline uses        21

22   linear interpolation of its own labels and propagates its actual exact slope bound. 22
23   Both evaluate the same continuous innovation law analytically and round labels to       23

24   dyadic multiples of 2−40 . All numerical query errors are outwardly enclosed and        24

25   enter the certificate.                                                                  25

26     All rungs are retained, including those preceding the first target crossing. The      26

27   clock to a target includes every preceding rung, target formation, rational com- 27
28   pilation, actor construction, verification, and durable checkpoint output. Fresh        28

29   processes run sequentially on one selected CPU with one numerical-library thread        29

30   and an identical non-economic warm-up. Imports and warm-up are excluded from            30

31   the internal clock but included in a separate process clock. CPU frequency is not       31

32   fixed. Independent policy-cost comparison is not performed in this catalogue and        32

30     The neural backend certifies all twelve services at 0.5 and 0.25, and nine of twelve                      30

31   at 0.125. The spline certifies all twelve at every target. The three neural failures are                    31

32   repetitions of the same horizon-four, price-four economy: the final gap is approxi- 32

1    mately 0.12503, just above 0.125. They remain failures rather than being rounded                1

2    into attainment. Across the 33 common successful method/target/repetition com- 2
3    parisons, the spline reaches the target earlier in every recorded observation. Thus             3

4    the new execution establishes reproducible complete construction and a transparent              4

5    frontier, not neural cost superiority. The deterministic termination corollary con- 5
6    cerns its specified sufficient budget; the predeclared finite ladder need not contain           6

7    that budget. The finite catalogue measures the deterministic backend just specified. 7
8    It neither overwrites the original neural optimizer’s attainment array nor turns the            8

9    seven retained-policy diagnostics into prospective observations. The theorem sup- 9
10   plies a method-level termination statement for this backend; the recorded services              10

11   establish only the achieved targets, costs, and storage on the declared economies. A            11

12   smaller certificate is not a lower realized policy cost. The new numerical comparison           12

13   and the earlier direct policy-cost comparisons therefore remain distinct throughout.            13

15                     8. NEURAL CONTINUATION WITH AN ACTION WITNESS                                 15

16                                                                                                   16
       A constructed continuation contains information about the action that produced
17                                                                                                   17
     each Bellman label. Discarding that information and subsequently controlling an
18                                                                                                   18
     unrelated actor can charge the same approximation twice. We now retain the label’s
19                                                                                                   19
     feasible action through the minimum circuit. This changes the returned policy, not
20                                                                                                   20
     merely the description of an old certificate. The construction continues to use the
21                                                                                                   21
     original economic primitives and its own fitted future.
22                                                                                                   22

                      8.4. Matched construction, policy and certificate frontiers
24                                                                                                24

25     The following matched catalogue is the frozen R46 experiment. Its statements               25

26   about direct policy cost refer to the evidence then available; Section 13 supplies           26

27   the new actual-policy comparison, and Section 12 executes the state-dependent                27

28   construction. The design was fixed in the repository before execution. It retains            28

29   the nonlinear continuous-innovation economy (27), horizons 2, 4, prices 1, 4, and            29

30   all six resolutions 16, 32, 64, 128, 256, 512. Three methods and three isolated timing       30

31   repetitions produce 36 services and 216 rungs. Each rung restarts from primitives. 31
32   The two cone methods have identical critics and node-action arrays; their deployed           32
                                     NEURAL BELLMAN OPERATORS                                         33

1    selectors differ. The spline constructs its own labels. All methods receive the same                   1

2    exact integration and outward numerical arithmetic.                                                    2

3      The declared targets are 1/4, 1/8 and 1/16. Every unsuccessful rung and every                        3

4    capped failure is retained. Internal prefix clocks include target construction, witness                4

5    or nearest-cell compilation, certificates and durable checkpoints; process clocks                      5

6    additionally include imports and the common non-economic warm-up. Runs use                             6

7    one selected CPU and one numerical-library thread, with method order rotated                           7

8    across repetitions and economic cells. Frequency is not controlled. These are local                    8

9    measurements, not a hardware-independent work theorem. Primitive counts and                            9

10   rational storage are recorded separately from clocks, and are not called complete                      10

11   floating-point or bit-operation counts.                                                                11

10                 9. STATE-DEPENDENT CONSTRAINTS AND FEASIBLE WITNESSES                            10

11     Investment opportunities often depend on current capital, collateral or remain- 11
12   ing capacity. The common-action construction above does not by itself authorize                12

13   transporting a node’s action to a state where that action is inadmissible. We now              13

14   incorporate a feasible repair into the same neural Bellman construction. The original          14

15   controlled economy, rather than a relaxed control problem, remains the comparison              15

16   object.                                                                                        16

17     Use the invariant state domain, numerical-query conditions and common evalua- 17
18   tion functional of Section 7. Replace its common action set by nonempty compact                18

19   sets At ( x ) satisfying                                                                       19

20                                                                                                  20

21
                                   d H ( At ( x ), At (z)) ≤ KtA ∥ x − z∥1 ,                (36)    21

22                                                                                                  22
     where d H is Hausdorff distance in the action norm. The primitive inequalities (17)
23                                                                                                  23
     are now required for pairs of feasible state–action points; neither costs nor dynamics
24                                                                                                  24
     need to be evaluated at infeasible actions. Assume effective finite k t -nets of At ( xt,i )
25                                                                                                  25
     and a feasible Borel repair Γt,i ( x ) ∈ At ( x ) of each stored action at,i , with
26                                                                                                  26

4      This result closes a constructive route for a class of endogenous constraints within   4

5    the original model. It does not assume that all feasible correspondences have a          5

6    finite Hausdorff modulus. With s = ε/ ∑tT=0 Bt , sufficient nonterminal allocations      6

7    are et ≤ s/8, DtQ k t ≤ s/4, Lt ht ≤ s/8 and DtQ ζ t ≤ s/4. Together with the earlier    7

8    terminal allocation, they imply finite termination at tolerance ε when all listed        8

9    operations are effective. Zero coefficients impose no restriction. When exact repair     9

10   is available, the larger query allowance of Corollary 9 is again sufficient. Setting     10

11   KtA = ζ t = 0 recovers the common-action witness theorem.                                11

       9.0.0.1. A capacity-constrained investment rule. For two nonnegative state coor- 13
14
     dinates on [0, 1]2 , let A( x ) = [0, ( x1 + x2 )/2]. This is a state-dependent capital- 14
15
     capacity constraint with K A = 1/2 in the ℓ1 state norm. The repair Γi ( x ) =           15

16
     min{ ai , ( x1 + x2 )/2} is feasible, satisfies (37) with ζ = 0, and has an elementary   16

17
     affine–ReLU representation for each fixed witness. Thus changing capacity changes        17

18
     the actor and the constructive state modulus through an explicit term D Q /2. The in- 18
19
     dex selector remains a finite comparison procedure. The supplement provides exact        19

20
     rational checks for this example; these are checks of the theorem and admissibility      20

21
     contract, not an additional multidimensional performance study.                          21

22
       Neither feasible-net construction nor repair is free. With Jt,i node actions, target- 22
23
     query work is summed over ∑i Jt,i rather than a common Kt Jt . Each deployed query       23

24
     also incurs the selected repair’s work and storage. Under imperfect state acquisition,   24

25
     repair must be certified feasible at the true state, or throughout the acquisition set   25

26
     for a robust implementation. An error allowance for the objective cannot substitute      26

27
     for that feasibility condition.                                                          27

                        10. FROM ACQUIRED STATES TO FEASIBLE POLICIES

30     A policy is implemented at an acquired state, not at an exact real number available    30

32   sented slope. In state dimension d, each computed cone score differs from its exact                        32

9      This implementation does not round a rational switchpoint and assume that its                  9

10   original cell is unchanged. For the scalar comparisons, all cells intersecting a state           10

11   enclosure are retained. For acquired constrained deployment, a numerical index has               11

12   the objective allowance (46) and its action is repaired against the entire acquisition           12

13   set. Both routes address discontinuity directly.                                                 13

15                            10.2. The identical non-neural envelope                                 15

16                                                                                                    16
       The envelope construction must be separated from the language used to encode it.
17                                                                                                    17
     The classical Lipschitz extension (McShane, 1934), a native minimum computation,
18                                                                                                    18
     and an exact ReLU realization can represent the same function.
19                                                                                                    19

20     P ROPOSITION 13—Representation control: Fix the node locations, labels, slopes,                20

21   original action witnesses, and selector tie rule. A conventional min-plus implementation         21

22   and the affine–ReLU implementation of the same envelope return the same mathematical             22

23   continuation and policy. They have the same exact policy certificate and direct policy cost. A   23

24   balanced realization uses O(dK ) arithmetic and comparison gates for K cones and depth           24

25   O(log K + log d). Merely changing the representation does not improve the covering or            25

26   Bellman-query factors.                                                                           26

27                                                                                                    27

28     P ROOF : For every pair of real numbers, min{ a, b} = a − ( a − b)+ . Applying                 28

29   this identity through a balanced reduction tree proves equality of the continuation. 29
30   Carrying the same attaining index, with the prescribed tie rule, proves equality                 30

31   of the action witness and repaired action. The certificates and costs concern these              31

32   mathematical objects. The gate count includes the coordinate absolute values and                 32
     40

1    their sums. The supplement separately bounds floating-point gate errors; exact                       1

2    equality is not asserted for unchecked rounded evaluations.                               Q.E.D.     2

3                                                                                                         3

4
       We execute this non-neural control on all 24 distinct scalar witness checkpoints. 4
5
     It shares the defining data rather than masquerading as an independently trained                     5

6
     competitor. The native selector and its ReLU encoding are thus one construction                      6

7
     with two representations. The learned hidden-location methods studied earlier                        7

8
     are different candidate generators. Their assessment, and that of the deterministic                  8

9
     backend, remains a comparison of actual policies and complete computational                          9

10
     work.                                                                                                10

                11. WITNESS-PRESERVING COMPILATION OF THE NEURAL OPERATOR
12                                                                                                        12

13     The cost of a Bellman construction depends on how its continuation is evaluated. 13
14   A literal evaluation of the cone network in Theorem 10 visits every state label at                   14

15   every action–innovation query. Repeating that scan is unnecessary on a tensor cover. 15
16   The following result compiles the same continuation and original action witnesses;                   16

17   it neither changes the fitted function nor substitutes a newly optimized policy.                     17

18     Let X = ∏dj=1 { x j,0 , . . . , x j,n j }, with strictly increasing coordinates and S = ∏ j (n j + 18
19   1). The rectangular domain is the product of the coordinate intervals. Index the                     19

20   nodes lexicographically. For arbitrary real labels yi and L ≥ 0, write                               20

21                                                                                                        21
          f ( x ) = min{yi + L∥ x − xi ∥1 },     i∗ ( x ) = min argmin{yi + L∥ x − xi ∥1 }.        (47)
22                i ≤S                                             i ≤S                                   22

23                                                                                                        23
     Each original index carries a feasible node action ai . Labels need not be Lipschitz-
24                                                                                                        24
     consistent: the numerical errors allowed in the earlier construction are permitted
25                                                                                                        25
     here without alteration. Define the transformed label and its original owner at a
26                                                                                                        26
     grid node v by
27                                                                                                        27

28                                                                                                        28
              zv = min{yi + L∥v − xi ∥1 },          ov = min argmin{yi + L∥v − xi ∥1 }.            (48)
                         i                                         i
29                                                                                                        29

31   formed value is stored.                                                                              31

3                                                                                                      3
                              f ( x ) = min {zv + L∥ x − v∥1 },                                 (49)

                                        lex

7    Forward and backward relaxations along each coordinate line compute all pairs (zv , ov )          7

8    using O(dS) additions and comparisons and O(S) stored values and indices. After cell              8

9    location, a value or exact witness query uses at most 2d corner pairs and O(d2d ) arithmetic      9

10   operations. A binary search locates the cell in O(∑ j log(n j + 1)) comparisons; a uniform        10

11   grid permits direct location.                                                                     11

12     When node coordinates, labels and L are rational, exact rational preprocessing and              12

13   comparisons implement these identities, including zero slopes, inconsistent labels and ties.      13

14   Applying the same repair map to the original returned action gives exactly the same feasible      14

15   policy and policy cost as the uncompiled construction. The residual and acquisition bounds        15

16   of Theorems 10 and 11 are unchanged.                                                              16
